2160. JANET STEWART was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of June, 1 pepper-eastor, value 1 s.; 1 salt-cellar, 1 s.; 1 printed book, 1 s.; and 1 knife, 1 s.; the goods of Robert Cole, her master.
ROBERT COLE . I am a pawnbroker, and live in East-street, Walworth; the prisoner was in my service, and left on the 19th of June. On the 20th of Oct. Eleanor Stewart came and told me something, which caused me to go to No. 2, Union-row—I there-found a book, pepper-castor, salt-cellar, and" knife of mine—they were given up to me by Eleanor Stewart—I then went with Baker to No. 8, Union-row, found the prisoner, and gave her into custody—these are the articles—they were safe in my house while the prisoner was with me.
ELEANOR STEWART . I am the prisoner's sister, and live at No. 2, Union-row. About four months ago I went to see my sister, while she was
living with Mr. Cole—she did not give me anything then—when she left her place I asked her for a few halfpence, as I was very badly off, and she gave me these things—she was living at home then, at No. 8, Union-row—it is about four months ago—I took them away—these are the same things—I delivered them to Mr. Cole—she asked me if my father had ever read this book—I said I did not know—she said, "Well, give it to him and let him read it"—that was after she had left Mr. Cole.
JAMES BAKER ( policeman.) I took the prisoner and told her the charge—she said it was all spite—next day, when locked up at the station I heard her say that she did give her sister the pepper-castor—she made a statement before the Magistrate, which was taken down—this is Mr. Henry's handwriting—( read)—"The prisoner says,—I lent the book to my father to read; my sister took it to him, and I intended her to bring it back, but I never could get it back, my father thinking it was my own; the other things I gave Eleanor to buy bread for herself and father, as she said they were in want of it."
Prisoner Defence. My sister came to me at Mr. Cole's one morning before Mr. Cole was up, and asked for some money, and something else to make away with; I said I had nothing; she went to the cupboard and saw this pepper-box and salt-cellar, and asked me for them; she laid them on the table; I said she might take them if she thought proper; she pledged them for 9 d., and made away with the money; four months afterwards she owed me a little spite, and she took them out of pledge, and took them to Mr. Cole.
ELEANOR STEWART re-examined. She gave them to me at home, at her mother's house—I will not be sure whether she had left her place or not.
MR. COLS re-examined. The witness in her former examination stated that she did come to my house, and took them from there.
ELEANOR STEWART re-examined. I signed this examination, but they had a solicitor, and he stupified me so that I did not know what I was saying—( The witness's deposition being read, stated,—"About four months ago I went to see my sister, the prisoner, when she was living with Mr. Cole, and she gave me a knife, pepper-castor, and salt-cellar, which I carried away.")
NOT GUILTY .

2162. JAMES WHITEMAN was indicted for unlawfully obtaining 5 dozen wheelbarrows, from Samuel Mills, by false pretences: also on two other charges for obtaining goods from John Bennet and Thomas Gurney, by false pretences, to all which he pleaded

[trialAccount t18451027-2163]
2163. MATTHEW HENRY CHILLINGWORTH was indicted for embezzlement.
MR. ROBINSON conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN HAY . I am the keeper of the Surrey Asylum. I was indebted
to Mr. Bennett 5 l. 7 s.—I sent my man with a check of 11 l. 5 s. to pay that—this is the check—my man came back—I received 5 l. 18 s. in change—that was the amount I ought to have received if the amount I owed to Mr. Bennet had been paid, with the exception of 2 1/2 d., which they generally throw off—I have no recollection when I sent the check—it is dated on the 1st of Aug., and I had had it by me some two or three days before I sent it—one Sunday morning, a week or two afterwards, the prisoner came with Harris, who makes out Mr. Bennett's bills—Harris said in the prisoner's presence that there was something wrong about a sovereign that was paid short—he asked me what I had paid—I told him I had given Sharpe, my man, a check for 11 l. 5 s., and when be came back he brought me 5 l. 18 s., and the bill receipted—this is the bill.
JOHN SHARPE . I am servant to Mr. Hay—on the 4th of Aug. he sent me with a check—I do not know whether this is the check—it was wrapped in a bit of paper—what Mr. Hay gave me I took to Mr. Bennett's—I gave the check to the prisoner—I did not tell him anything—he got change and gave me the change and this bill—I took it back to my master—there were five sovereigns, and some silver—I did not count the silver.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Had you any conversation with the prisoner about the amount? A. No, I merely gave him the check in a piece of paper—I counted the sovereigns I received back, but not the silver—I just took what he gave me—I was in Mr. Bennett's shop when I gave the prisoner the check—there were one or two people in the shop—I do not know where he got change for the check—he went out to get it, and came the way of the London-road.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. Whatever money the prisoner gave you, you carried to your master? A. Yes—I carried it all the way in my hand.
DEBORAH BENNETT . I am the wife of Solomon Bennett—I recollect, on the 4th of Aug., the prisoner gave me 4 l. 7 s., and said Mr. Hay bad paid his bill—it might have been about eleven or twelve o'clock in the day—the prisoner then went to the book, and I saw him writedown 4 l. 7 s.—this is the book, and this is his handwriting, "Hay paid bill, 4 l. 7 s."
Cross-examined. Q. In whose handwriting is this bill and receipt? A. The items I cannot say—my husband makes out the bills sometimes, and sometimes Harris does—I think this is my husband's writing—my sight is very bad—Mr. Hay owed 5 l. 7 s., and the prisoner gave me 4 l. 7 s.—I did not know the amount Mr. Hay owed—I am not always in the shop—I know the prisoner brought an action against us.
NATHAN HARRIS . I have been book-keeper to Mr. Bennett for many years—this bill is my writing—I make out all the bills—Mrs. Bennett knows nothing about them—this receipt to it is the prisoner's writing—on Sunday, the 10th of Aug., I went to the prosecutor's books, as I do every Sunday—no one writes in the ledger but myself—here is the common waste book, and everything that occurs is entered in this—here is another book, in which the entry is made from that common waste-book, and in this it is entered 5 l., in the prisoner's writing—this is for me to enter into the ledger—this is 5 l. only, but no shillings; that led to tne finding it out—here is, "Hay paid bill, 5 l."
JURY. Q. If you had got the 5 l., you would have been 7 s. short? A. Yes—that caused the finding it out—I said it must have been 5 l. 7 s., and then it was only 4 l. 7 s.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. What did you do then? A. I went to Mr. Hay, to know what had been paid, and then the prisoner said to me, "Don't say anything to mistress about it; I will try and make it up by next Sunday, and then the books will be all right"—he said he was sorry for it.
Cross-examined. Q. Will you swear that this 5 l. is the prisoner's writing? A. Decidedly—if it had been entered 5 l. 7 s., I should not have discovered it—I should have taken it for granted that the money had been paid.
SIMON BENNETT . I am the prosecutor's son—he is on the Continent—in consequence of what I heard from Harris, I had some conversation with the prisoner—I asked him what he had received from Mr. Hay—he said, "A cheque for 11 l. 5 s."—I said "How much did you give my mother?" he said 4 l. 7 s., and he gave Mr. Hay's man 5 l. 18 s.—I said, "How much do you make that?"—he said, "11 l. 5 s."—I said, "How do you make that out? it is only 10 l. 5 s."—he did not know what to say—he seemed very much confused, and did not seem to know how to account for the other 1 l.—his father was going by—I called him in and told him of it—he said, "I am very sorry, Mr. Bennett, but I am not surprised at it; he is a very bad boy; I could have got him into the Post Office"—the prisoner said he did not know how to account for the 1 l.; he must have lost it, and had reckoned it up wrong—I said I did not know what to do in it myself, as my father was on the Continent, and I was not in the business—I consulted with Mr. Harris, and we sent the prisoner away—next morning he entered an action against us—I believe the action was not brought by the prisoner's present attorney—I do not know.
JAMES LEWIS ( police-constable L 36). I apprehended the prisoner on the 11th of Aug.—he was given into custody by Harris—he said it was no use, he intended to bring an action against him. On the following day, in going to the police-office, he was met by an acquaintance of his own, who asked him what he had been doing, that he was locked up—he said Bennett's people had locked him up last night—he told him to go and get a solicitor—when he got a little farther, he met another person, who asked him what he was locked up for—he said he had made a mistake in a sovereign, and Bennett's people had locked him up.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18451027-2164]
2164. ELLEN LAVIE was indicted for unlawfully obtaining 2 half-crowns by false pretences.
SUSANNAH HARRIET TAPSON . I am the wife of John Tapson, a surgeon, living at Claphamrise—on the 10th of June, about half-past one o'clock, I saw the prisoner at my house—Mr. Tapson was from home—she said, "I am unknown to you, but well known to Mr. Tapson, who is well known to myself and my family; he has attended me and my family"—she said she was unfortunately situated with a street cab, and she had left home without her purse—I asked her what she required—she said 5 s. would be sufficient—I then save her two half-crowns, believing her to be a patient of my husband's, who, she said, had attended herself and family—she said she had been attended by my husband—I asked her to leave her name—she said she had no card, but would write her name—she wrote it, but I have destroyed it—she wrote, "Miss Evans, Grove House, Leyton, Essex"—I immediately replied, "I thought your name was Edwards"—she said, "No,
Evans is my name—Miss Evans"—she said she would return and leave it in two days—I begged she would call before eleven or after two o'clock, that she might see Mr. Tapson—she said she knew those were his hours, she knew him very well—she then left, and I did not see her again till she was in custody.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Are you sure you gave her two half-crowns? A. Yes, two half-crown pieces.
JOHN TAPSON . I am the husband of the last witness—I know nothing of the prisoner.
Cross-examined. Q. How long hare you been in business in Clapham? A. Between four and five years—I succeeded to a practice previous to that, and before that I had been in the hospital—I can say that I never attended the prisoner—I should remember the face of every person I attended—I attend all my cases myself—if I have been obliged to send an assistant I have known the cases he has seen—I will undertake to say that I know every person I hate attended both in the hospital and out of it—I have been in practice about ten years from the time I went into the hospital—I cannot speak to the number of patients I have attended—I think not so many as 200 or 800 a year—I will undertake to say I remember all those to a sufficient extent to know that I never attended the prisoner—I cannot tell you that the prisoner has a particular eye or a particular note by which I know that I have not attended her, but if I had attended you I should remember it—I have patients of the name of Edwards and Evans—I know them perfectly—I have not looked through my books with reference to this case—I had my attention called to the prisoner for the first time when she was at the police court—Mr. Saunders had my business before me.
COURT. Q. You are able to swear that the prisoner was not a patient of yours? A. That she was not a patient, or ever bad been.
RICHARD COVER ( police-constable P 88.) I apprehended the prisoner on the 3rd Oct. on another charge.
GUILTY — Confined Six Months.
(There were two other charges against the prisoner.)
[trialAccount t18451027-2165]
2165. BENJAMIN HENRY HOLYOAK was indicted for stealing 6 planes, value 1 l. 15 s.; 3 saws, 15 s.; and 1 stock, 15 s.; the goods of William Ingliss; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
WILLIAM INGLISS . I am a carpenter, in the employ of Mr. Young, of West-square, St. Georges's. On the 2nd of Sept. I was working at a building in New-street, Bermondsey New-road—I went away to dinner at twelve o'clock, leaving my tools lying about—there were three baskets there, one of which was mine, but the tools were not in it—after I bad been away about half an hour, Green called us out of a public-house—I went back with him, and found the greater part of my tools in the basket, and in charge of Ashworth—there were six planes, a stock, and three saws—they were worth about 3 l. altogether—they are here—I never knew the prisoner.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. Were there a great number of tools scattered about? A. Yes—all that were in the basket were not mine—there were a few besides mine—there were three carpenters at work there
CHARLES GREEN . I am a labourer, and live in Blackfriars-row. on the 2nd of Sept. I was employed by Mr. Young at the buildmg—I remember
the men going to dinner—I remained outside to have an eye to the bricklayers' tools—the prisoner was not employed there, but I saw him come round to the corner of the building where I sat having my dinner—I merely saw his face—I did not see whether he had anything or not—he said to me, "Where are the carpenters gone to dinner who work at this building?"—I said, "I do not know"—he walked off to the front of the building—in a few minutes I thought he had no business round there, as it was not a public thoroughfare, and I jumped up, took my dinner in my hand, and went round to the front of the building—in consequence of what a woman told me I followed the prisoner, and overtook him with the basket of tools on his back—I said, "Where are you going with these tools, mate?"—he said, "I am going to a fresh job"—I said, "I am Yery much mistaken if you have not taken these from our building"—he laid, "God bless you! I will go back with you"—he did so—I unlocked the back door—he went into the building and put the tools down—Mr. Ashworth said to me, "Is it the men's tools he has got?"—I said, "I do not know, he seems to say they are his own"—"They are my own," the prisoner said—I said to Mr. Ashworth, "Do you know how many baskets the carpenters have got?"—he said, "Three large ones, and one small one"—I went into every room, and could only find two large ones and one small one—I said to the prisoner, "You had better stop till the men come from dinner"—he said, "I shall lose my job; I know where the men go to dinner; I will go and make it all right that they are mine"—he then left the tools in the building and said to Mr. Ashworth, "I will leave my tools in your charge while I go to the men, to satisfy them that the tools are mine"—the prisoner and I then walked off to the Compasses, where the men dine—when we got nearly there he said, "You go in and tell the men to come out"—I said, "You had better come in with me"—he went with me. very near to the door, and took a pipe out of his pocket—he said, "Go in and tell them to come out"—I left him outside and went in, tapped the prosecutor on the shoulder, and said, "I want you"—I then went out, and the prisoner was gone—I am quite certain he is the man—he was dressed in a white flannel jacket.
Cross-examined. Q. You had not seen him before? A. Never—I saw him again when I was sent for on the Monday following—this occurred on Tuesday, the 2nd of Sept.—I merely saw his face when I first saw him—I could not tell whether he had anything on his back or not—I cannot exactly say what room the carpenters' tools were in—I did not know what was in the basket till I got up to him—I did not examine them—I do not know that they were the same that were in the basket when I came back—the prisoner did not offer me anything to drink or smoke—the house where the men got their dinner might be three or four minute's walk from the building—the prisoner and I walked there side by side—Robert Everett was one of the carpenters—the name of the other I do not know—those men are at work there still—the prisoner did not tell me he had been sent for some carpenter's tools—he said the tools were his—I said, "The prisoner seems to say they are his," and he assented to it—when I saw him at the police-office it was not on this charge.
ROBERT ASHWORTH . I am a hatter, and live in Brook-street, Bermondsey New-road. On Tuesday, the 2nd of Oct, between twelve and one o'clock, my wife told me something, and I went to the building—I saw the prisoner and Green—the prisoner had a basket of carpenter's
tools on his back—there was some talk between us about these tools—the prisoner left them in my charge, and said he would go with Green and make it all right—I took charge of them till Green and the carpenters came back—the prisoner did not come back—I showed Ingliss the same tools and the basket—I had had charge of them all the time.
Cross-examined. Q. When you came in was the basket lying down? A. No; the prisoner had it on his back, and Green was with him in the front part of the building—Green said the prisoner seemed to say that the tools were his own; and the prisoner said they were his, and he was come to look for a job—the conversation occupied from five till, I suppose, ten minutes—I never saw the prisoner before—I saw him at the station-house nearly a fortnight afterwards—I swear he is the person.
MART EVANS . I am the wife of Evan Evans, a tanner—I saw the prisoner between twelve and one o'clock on the 2nd of Sept from the back part of the building with a basket on his shoulder—I called out to a bricklayer's labourer on the premises—he went after the prisoner—I saw him overtake him, and bring him back—I am sure he is the person.
Cross-examined. Q. How far was he from you? A. I was at my gate—he came from the back of the building, and came towards me—he had passed me before he was brought back—the nearest point at which he had passed me was twenty yards, or perhaps more—I saw him when he came back with the basket.
ROBERT ASH WORTH re-examined. This is the basket which the prisoner had on his back, and these are the tools—I kept them till Ingliss came.
WILLIAM INGLISS re-examined. These tools are mine—here are planes, saws, and a stock.
JOHN BARKER ( police-constable K 108.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction at Clerkenwell—( read—Convicted 20 th May, 1845, and confined three months)—I was at the trial—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY .— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18451027-2166]
2166. DANIEL WILLIAMS was indicted for stealing 1 coat, value 4 l.; 1 waistcoat, 18 s.; and 1 pair, of trousers, 1 l. 16 s.; the goods of James Charles Edmiston and another.
GEORGE HOBDAY . I am partner with James Charles Edmiston—we are tailors, and live in Gracechurch-street—the prisoner came to our shop about the 30th of Sept.—I do not recollect seeing him before that day—he said, "I want a suit of black; my name is Williams, 43, Mincing-lane; you have done something for me before"—I said, "I recollect the name, but not the face"—I took his instructions for making a suit of black, and asked the day it was to go home—he said on Friday, at four o'clock—he asked the price; I said, "4 l. the coat, 18 s. the waistcoat, and 1 l. 16 s. the trousers"—he said, "What will that be?"—I said, "6 l. 14 s., but I suppose 6 l. 10 s. will do for cash"—he said it was for cash—the clothes were made, and sent by our shopman, Charles Hemmings—he returned, but brought back nothing except the change which had been given him to take with the things—I saw the prisoner afterwards at the Lambeth police-station.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. He called your attention to a transaction you had had with him some time before? A. Yes—I
recollect it—it was a ready money transaction—he fetched the article, and paid 16 s. for it—that was the transaction he reminded me of—he said, "My name is Williams, 43, Mincing-lane;" and he went on to say, "That is our place of business, but you had better send to my private address," and I did so—our shopman went to 43, Mincing-lane—I have ascertained there is a place of business there conducted by the prisoner's father, but the father said he had not been there for five months—the clothes were sent back a fortnight afterwards, on the day on which we appeared before Mr. Henry—that was not within three days of the transaction—there was a remand for a week—the prisoner had the clothes on in court, to the Magistrate's surprise—Charles Hemmings has been in our employ for twelve months.
JAMES CHARLES EDMISTON . I am in partnership with Mr. Hobday. I only knew of this order from the order-book—I sent the clothes home by our shopman—I gave him particular instructions not to leave them without the money—there was a black coat, waistcoat, and trowsers—I gave the shopman change for a 10 l. note—he came back about half-past five o'clock, and stated he had left the clothes—I went with him to the address immediately—I took a policeman with me, and went in a cab —I did not find the prisoner—I went again on Saturday morning—I did not find the prisoner then—I then went to the police-office, and asked the Magistrate's advice—I did not see the prisoner till he was in custody—I had never seen him before.
Cross-examined. Q. When your shopman came back without either clothes or money, you were very angry with him? A. No, I was not angry, nor quite pleased—I did not tell him that unless the money was got he should go out of my service—I never used any threat of that kind—I did not tell him I would not keep him—I did not give him warning—I did not say anything about the clothes, except that we were to go back and endeavour to find the man—I did not go or send to Mincing-lane before we supplied the clothes—the prisoner had said he lived in Dorey-street—I certainly went there that evening with the cab —I did not see Mr. Matthews that night—I went again the next morning—I did not see Mr. Matthews then—I saw him at St. Martin's workhouse—he was holding some office in the parish, and was landlord of the house where the prisoner was living—he said he considered the prisoner was perfectly respectable—I inquired if he had absconded, and Mr. Matthews said he had slept at home every night—I inquired about his employment—it is quite possible that Mr. Matthews said, "I have no doubt he will pay you, if you wait a day or two"—I do not recollect it now—if I said so at the Police-court, it was true—I do not recollect that I said in answer to that, "Very well, I will"—I will not swear I did not say so—I said, "I will endeavour to see you again before I take harsh measures"—I said that because I would not frighten the man away from his lodging, but with the intention of taking him into custody—I should not have taken him into custody if I had been paid.
Q. If Mr. Matthews had paid you the money on that occasion, would you or would you not have given the prisoner into custody? A. Certainly I should—I should not have taken the money from Mr. Matthews—if I said, "Very well, I will wait," it was a subterfuge, and I was telling a lie, because I would not frighten the man away—I went to Mr. Matthews to
ascertain if he was a respectable man, as I doubted whether the house was respectable—I wished to discover every little testimony I could—I did not go to Mincing-lane—it struck me I might have got some information there, but if I had gone there I might have been in the situation of compounding a felony—when I called at Dorey-street I did not see Mrs. Matthews—I saw two or three women—I saw a woman whom I have since ascertained to be Mrs. Matthews—I do not know that I saw this lady—( looking at Mrs. Matthews)—I swear I had not a long conversation with her—I have no recollection of her face at all—I believe it was a boy opened the door to me—I saw three women there—I asked if Mr. Williams was at home—one of the women did not say no, but she expected him in shortly—if that had been said I should have waited—I think the boy asked me to walk in and sit down—I had a policeman with me in uniform.
CHARLES HEMMINGS . I am shopman to the prosecutor. I saw the prisoner come into the shop—I did not hear the bargain about the clothes—I was sent home with the clothes—I was to take them home, and not leave them without the money—when I got to Dorey-street I did not find the prisoner—I was told to wait—I found the prisoner did live there—I waited about twenty minutes—it was about five o'clock when I got there—the prisoner came in, and asked if I came from Mr. Edmiston—I said I did—he said, "You have brought my clothes, have you?"—I said, "Yes"—he said he would go up stairs and put them on—he took them from me and went up stairs—he came down, and had them on—he asked me if there could be any alteration made in the breast of the coat—I said it could, but it was not usual in that kind of coat—he then looked down, and said they would do very well—he said, "I have just come from Mr. Edmiston and have settled for them, and he sent word you are to leave them"—he said he had made an arrangement if there was any alteration wanted to have it done on Tuesday—I said I had got the bill and was told to bring back the money, I did not know what to do—I said, "Have you anything to show?"—he said it was all right, and he would call and let Mr. Edmiston see them on.
cross-examined. Q. When you got home whom did you see? A. Mr. Hobday and Mr. Edmiston were in the shop—Mr. Edmiston was very angry with me for having left the clothes—he did not swear at me—he did not tell me he should not keep me in his service—he did not give me warning—he exclaimed he was robbed—he and Mr. Hobday were very angry—I am still in the place, and have had had no warning to quit—I do not know that I have any reason to expect to be turned away—Mrs. Matthews was not present at the time I had the conversation with the prisoner about the clothes—there was no woman at all in the room—I could not tell whether Mrs. Mathews wai in the adjoining room—I can tell every word that the prisoner said when he came down stairs—I know what he said, and the place in wnich every word stood, without the alteration of a word.
HENRY NOLAN ( police-constable L 129.) The prisoner was given into my custody on the 6th of Oct. by Hemmings—the prisoner wanted him to go into the City with him, and said be would settle with him—he would not—he said he was afraid he would make his escape.
MR. BALLANTINE called
MRS. MATTHEWS. I am the wife of Samuel Matthews, who it relieving
overseer of the parish of St. Martin-in-the-field—the prisoner has lodged at our house about four or five months—I have not the slightest fault to find with him—he has been a decent, and, as far as I have seen, an honest young man—I remember some clothes being brought home on the 3rd of Oct. by Hemmings—I opened the door, and asked him to come in and wait—the prisoner came home soon afterwards—I left the room instantly, and went into the next room—I could hear every word that passed—I recollect the prisoner coming down with the clothes on—he made great complaints about the coat not fitting him on the breast—I considered it was the coat by his saying the breast, and he said he would call and make arrangement with Mr. Edmiston on Tuesday about the alteration—he did not say, in my hearing, that he had been to Mr. Edmiston's, and that it was all right—I heard all that passed till within a minute, when I left the room, went to the back-door, and then the street-door shut—the prisoner did not say he had been to Mr. Edmiston's—he said he would go to him—Hemmings did not object to that in my hearing—I did not hear him speak at all—the prisoner has been at my house and slept there every night—he has generally been in about ten o'clock at night—he was there to be found then, and at eight o'clock in the morning—he always paid his lodging regularly.
COURT. Q. You say you were away from the next room? A. Yes, about a minute.
MR. EDMISTON. Will you ask the witness who paid the rent to her? Witness. The prisoner never paid it at all, his father paid it.
MR. BALLANTINE ( to the witness). Q. Did you always get your rent? A. Yes—I know the prisoner's father—he is a coffee-broker, in Mincing-lane—I should consider he is a respectable man.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18451027-2167]
2167. MATILDA PALMER was indicted for stealing 1 diamond, value 8 s.; 1 ruby, 7 s.; 1 purse, 1 s.; and 4 shillings; the goods and monies of Thomas Gardner, from his person.
THOMAS GARDNER . I am an oilman, and live at Camberwell. On the 21st of Oct., I was in the New Kent-road—the prisoner accosted me—I have no doubt she is the person—I did not want to say anything to her—she put her arms round me, and I felt her hands in each of my trowsers' pockets—I had a frock coat on, but it was open—she took her hands out of my pockets and ran away—I directly felt that she had taken my purse out—I caught her shawl, and said, "Stop a minute"—she ran off, and left her shawl in my hand—I hallooed out, and two gentlemen and a policeman came up—I had in my pocket a diamond, a ruby, and a purse with 4 s. in it—my trowsers were rather low down.
Prisoner. I declare I never saw him; I had been washing all day; I was never out of the house all day; I never had such a shawl in my life.
JOHN DAVID STEADMAN . I am a policeman. I was on duty about two o'clock that morning—I had seen the prisoner on the road several times that night previously, and about two minutes before the robbery, I saw her speak to the prosecutor—I went on about forty yards—I then saw her run down Lyon-street, and saw the prosecutor with this shawl lying at his feet—I know the shawl—I had seen the prisoner wearing it often, and had seen it on her not two minutes before—I went to her
house and took her—she denied the robbery, and denied knowing the shawl—I had seen her six or seven times that evening.
Prisoner. As to your seeing me that evening, you might have seen me at eight o'clock; not afterwards.
GUILTY .* Aged 19.— Confined Nine Months.
[trialAccount t18451027-2167a]
2167. THOMAS FOUNTAIN was indicted for stealing 1 handker-chief, value 1 s. 6 d., the goods of Parlet Patrick, from his person; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
PARLET PATRICK . I live in York-street, York-road, Lambeth. On the 12th of Oct, I was walking with my wife in York-road, about a quarter past nine o'clock—the prisoner came behind me very close, and I made room for him to pass—he did not pass, but turned round and walked the other way—after I had walked about twenty yards farther, he came behind me again—I then had my thoughts about me—I looked over my shoulder, and he had got hold of my coat—I turned, and caught him—I said, "Young man, stop"—he ran off—I ran after him, down Sutton-street, calling, "Stop thief!" there were some men at the end—the prisoner then sung out "Stop him!"—I said, "That is him," and they stopped him—he threw open his coat, and said, "I have got nothing of yours"—the policeman then came and took him—he searched, and found nothing—he said, "We will go back"—he found my handkerchief about ten yards from where the prisoner first ran from—a little boy had picked it up—it is mine, and I had lost it from my pocket.
Prisoner. Q. Can you swear I am the man? A. Yes—I never lost sight of you—I did not see you pick my pocket, or throw the handker-chief away—I did not see it in your possession, nor find it on you.
EDWARD O'HARA . I heard a cr'y of "Stop thief!"—I ran out of No. 30, Sutton-street, and saw the prisoner running—I followed him—I did not see anything thrown away—in coming back I picked up a pocket handkerchief.
PARLET PATRICK re-examined. The prisoner had run by the place where the handkerchief was found.
JAMES SARTAIN ( police-constable L 101.) I took the prisoner—when I had him in custody, the handkerchief was handed to me, on the same side of the way.
Prisoner. I was going along, and turned down Sutton-street; a young man rushed by me with great force; I heard a cry of "Stop thief!" I cried too, and the roan stopped me; I said, "I have got nothing;" I can assure you I am quite innnocent.
EDWARD O'HARA re-examined. Q. Who was running first? A. The prisoner—I was in front of the mob—I saw them coming up—they passed me—I did not hear the prisoner cry." Stop thief!"—I heard the prosecutor.
JAMES BROOK ( policeman.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, at Newington—( read—Convicted 21 th July, 1844)—he was confined one year, because he used great violence when taken—he is the man.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18451027-2168]
2168. HENRY MARKS was indicted for stealing 9 1/2 yards of printed cotton, value 5 s., the goods of William Buddry Elsden; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
HENRY SPARLING . I am shopman to William Buddry Elsden, of Brunswick-place, Old Kent-road. On the 30th of Sept. this piece of printed cotton was safe in the shop at ten minutes past eight o'clock—it was missed at half-past eight—I am quite sure this is it—it is my master's.
WILLIAM NOAKES ( police-constable M 104.) On the evening of the 30th of Sept., at twenty minutes past eight o'clock, I stopped the prisoner about 300 yards from the prosecutor's—I asked what he had got—he said, "Nothing"—I opened his coat, and found this cotton under it—I said, "Do you call this nothing?"—he said, "That is what I just picked up."
Prisoner. I picked it up against the Railway station.
GEORGE WILDE ( police-constable M 94.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, at the Surrey Sessions—( read—Convicted 26 th May, 1845; confined one month, and whipped)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined One Year.

2169. MARGARET ROBERTS was indicted for stealing 1 watch, value 2 l.; and 1 pair of ear-rings, 5 s.; the goods of Ann Naylor, her mistress.
ANN NAYLOR . I am a widow. The prisoner was my charwoman—about the 27th of Sept. I was moving some goods, and missed a watch—I afterwards missed some other things—the watch has not been found—she said she had not seen it—here are some other things which are mine, but they are not in this indictment.
HENRY NIXON ( police-constable L 85.) I was called by the prosecutrix, and went after the prisoner—I asked if she knew anything of the watch—she said she did not—I took her to the station, and asked if she lodged at the house where I heard she did—she said, "No"—I searched there, and found thirty-eight duplicates—a breadtray, a looking-glass, and copper, were given me by Mrs. Reed—she is not here.
Prisoner. The duplicates under the bed were of my own property.
NOT GUILTY .

2170. MARGARET ROBERTS was again indicted for stealing 3 quilts, value 6 s.; 3 yards of crape, 1 s.; 1 pair of snuffers and tray, 2 s.; and 1 table-cloth, 3 s.; the goods of Ann Naylor, her mistress.
ANN NAYLOR . About the 20th of Jan. I missed some quilts, crape, snuffers, table-cloth, and other things—this quilt, and these other things, are mine—the prisoner was living with me as charwoman.
Prisoner. The two shifts belong tome. Witness. No, they are mine—I examined them at the pawnbroker's—I have no doubt about this counter-pane—these towels are mine—they are not marked, but I have some similar to them—the shirt is my son's—it is marked.
HENRY NIXON ( police-constable L 85.) I found the duplicates of the sheet and counterpane under the prisoner's bed, with other duplicates.
CHRISTOPHER DRY . I am assistant to a pawnbroker—I have the sheet and counterpane, which were pawned, I believe, by the prisoner—this is the duplicate given for them.
Prisoner. The prosecutrix was moving, to deprive the landlord of his rent; the shifts belong to me; the knives are mine, and one table-cloth; she allowed me to pawn some things.
ANN NAYLOR re-examined. I never allowed her to pawn this sheet or table-cloth—she never pawned but once in her life for me.
GUILTY . Aged 37.— Confined Four Months.

2171. WILLIAM MALEY was indicted for stealing 1 sovereign, 1 shilling, and 1 sixpence; the monies of Ann Elizabeth Rolfe, from her person; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
ANN ELIZABETH ROLFE . I am a servant—I live in Duke's-court, Union-street, Borough—on the 4th of Oct., at half-past eleven o'clock at night, I was standing on the pavement, where there were some people looking at a man—the officer told me something—I put my hand into my pocket and missed a sovereign, a shilling, and a sixpence, which I had had safe not a minute before, wrapped in a small piece of paper—I cannot swear that I saw the prisoner.
ROBERT BRANSFORD ( police-constable M 101.) I was on duty in Union-street, in the Borough—I saw the prisoner lift the prosecutrix's dress about a foot from the ground—I saw his hand in her pocket-hole—I watched him—one of his companions told him of it, and he left her—I went and spoke to her, and he disappeared—I took him about an hour afterwards.
Prisoner. I was going home that Saturday night, about eleven o'clock; I passed by a crowd; I was going home again, and the officer came and said I had picked a lady's pocket.
JAMES BOCKHAM ( police-sergeant L 37.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction at the Surrey Sessions—( read—Convicted 18 th March, 1844, confined twenty-one days, solitary)—I know him to be the person—he has been in custody five times besides.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18451027-2172]
2172. DAVID ROWELL was indicted for stealing 1 loaf of bread, value 5 d. the goods of Jonas Rowley, his master, since deceased.
EMILY REBECCA ROWLEY . I resided with my father, Jonas Rowley—he was a baker—I have been accustomed to transact his business—the prisoner was in his employ—I kept an account of what the prisoner delivered to Mrs. Freeman—I got the account from the prisoner—I copied it in the waste book, and then it was copied in the day-book—on the 7th of June the prisoner had one quartern loaf for Mrs. Freeman—it used not to be paid for—it was booked—he ought to have delivered that one loaf and he did not.
Cross-examined by MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. You only recollect it from the entry in the book? A. I made the entry in the waste book—my father used to copy it in the ledger, and it is in the ledger now—it is from seeing it in the book that I know I gave it him on the 7th of June—I could not charge my memory with it—the waste books were always destroyed as soon as they were filled up and entered in the ledger—I made a great many entries in a day—the account used to be given to me after the prisoner had taken the bread—Mrs. Freeman only had bread twice a week—I went before the Magistrate on this case on the 25th of August—nobody served in my father's shop but him and me—my mother was confined.
COURT. Q. Are you able from recollection to say you gave him this one loaf on the 7th of June? A. Yes—Mrs. Freeman had her loaves on Tuesdays and Saturdays—I am able to state that I did, on Saturday the 7th of June, give the prisoner one loaf for her—I never compared the day-book with the waste book, but I can, from my own memory, state I gave him one loaf for her on the 7th of June.
ANN FREEMAN . I dealt with Mr. Rowley for bread—I am sure the
prisoner did not bring me any bread on the 7th of June, because when he did I put it down on a slate.
NOT GUILTY .
(There were three other charges against the prisoner, on which no evidence was offered.)
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18451027-2173]
2173. MARY BROWN was indicted for stealing 1 pair of shoes, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of Samuel Beale and another.
SAMUEL BEALE . I am in partnership with Mr. Webster; we are pawnbrokers, and live in Great Charlotte-street, Blackfriars. On Saturday night, the 4th of Oct., the prisoner was brought into my shop on a charge, and this pair of shoes was produced—they are mine.
Prisoner. I went to buy a pair, and tried them on; I had one shoe on my foot when the policeman came and took me away; the young man who was going to have the money for them did not come to the Magistrate's on the following Monday; I did not see the prosecutor at the shop.
THOMAS WILLIAM REDFORD ( police-constable L 52.) I watched on the prosecutor's premises. I saw the prisoner come up to the front of the shop, take up a pair of shoos, and look round her—she then placed them in her lap—she went past the young man, who was serving a customer with an umbrella—I, thinking she was going off with them, took her, and said, "Where are you going with these shoes?"—she kicked off her shoe and said, "To try them on"—she had got two or three yards from the shop, going away, and had turned away from the shop.
Prisoner's Defence. I had the money in my hand to pay for them; I was trying them on, when the policeman came and took me, before I knew the price; the policeman said I was two yards from the door, which, is false; I was holding my foot up for the young man to put the shoe on.
Witness. No—she had 4 s. and some pence screwed up in a paper—she came up with some vegetables and other things in her lap—she placed a cabbage by the side of a post, took the shoes, then took the cabbage up, and was walking clear off—she drew the shoes from her lap, threw them down before her, kicked her shoe off, and commenced fumbling, trying to work her foot into one of the shoes.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18451027-2174]
2174. ROBERT BERRY and FRANCIS M'CARTHY were inindicted for unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin; to which
M'CARTHY pleaded GUILTY . Aged 19.—
Confined One Year.
MESSRS. SCRIVEN and CLARK conducted the Prosecution.
CHARLES BURGESS GOFF ( police-sergeant L 8.) I was on duty in Walcot-place, Lambeth, on the 29th of Sept., with Brook—I saw the prisoners—I had known M'Carthy by sight before—they were going in the direction of Kennington-common—I followed them through the New-road to Denmark-hill, Camberwell—they went down a turning leading to Camberwell-grove—I saw Berry go into a green-grocer's shop—I spoke to the female in the shop, and then followed the prisoners again—they went on to Cambcrwell-grcen, and stopped several minutes, and I saw M'Carthy give something to Berry—they separated, and Berry went into
Mrs. Godfrey's shop, which is a tobacconist's, and M'Carthy waited on the opposite side of the way—Berry came out, joined M'Carthy, and they went down the road—I went into Mrs. Godfrey's shop, and spoke to her—I came out again, and saw the prisoners in company on the opposite side of the way—I followed them to a confectioner's shop between the Green and Camberwell-gate—Berry went in there—when he came out I went in for about a minute—I overtook them again when I got to Walworth-road—when they got to Hampton-street they sat down, and waited four or five minutes—Berry then waited at the corner—M'Carthy went down the street, and took up something from the road, and rubbed something between his fingers—he then joined Berry, and gave him what he had rubbed—they went up the road towards the Elephant and Castle-Berry went into Mrs. Cross's shop, and M'Carthy crossed the road to the opposite side—I saw Brook take hold of him, and I went into the shop—as I went in, Berry threw his head back—I thought he was going to swallow something—I took hold of him, and in the struggle I saw this shilling in his hand—I said, "This is a counterfeit shilling"—he said he was going to give 1 d. for it—that was for the tobacco that Mrs. Cross was weighing—in going to the station, Brook was on ahead with M'Carthy, and Berry, pointing to M'Carthy, said, "That boy gave me the bad shilling"—the shilling I took from Berry had been rubbed with dirt.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. Were you on duty? A. Yes, in plain clothes—I did not know Berry—I had been following them nearly three hours—when M'Carthy went down Hampton-street I was opposite the corner of the street—he went ten or twelve paces up the street—I was sometimes fifty yards from them, but when there was anything material to be seen, I got as close to them as I could.
JAMES BROOK ( police-constable L 118). I was with Goff on the 29th of Sept.—I know Mrs. Bicknell's shop—I saw Berry go into that shop, and M'Carthy was on the opposite side of the way—I was afterwards in the neighbourhood of Mrs. Cross's shop—I secured M'Carthy nearly opposite to it—I found these two counterfeit shillings in his hand, they are bitten and cut—he made a resistance.
LOUISA GODFREY . I am the wife of George Godfrey, a tobacconist, in High-street, Camberwell. On the 29th of Sept, Berry came to the shop for a pennyworth of tobacco—he put down a counterfeit shilling—I took it up, bit it, and said it was not good, and he went away without the tobacco—he said he took the shilling on the Green—this is it—I bit it.
Cross-examined. Q. Which is the bite? A. Here on the edge of it—I did not make this bend on it.
MARY BICKNELL . I keep a tobacconist's shop in Regent-place, Walworth-road. Berry came there on the 29th of Sept for a pennyworth of tobacco—he offered me a shilling—I took it in my hand and cut two pieces out of it—this is it—I did not take it—he said I ought not to have done that, as he had just taken it down the road—he left the shop with it.
ELIZABETH CROSS . I am the wife of James Cross, a tobacconist, in Cumberland-row, Walworth-road. On the 29th of Sept. Berry came for a pennyworth of tobacco—he had not time to pay for it, as Mr. Goff rushed in and seized him.
CHARLES BURGESS GOFF re-examined. This is the shilling I got out of Berry's hand at Mrs. Cross's.
MR. JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of coin to the Mint. These three shillings are all counterfeit, and made of the same metal throughout—to my best opinion they are all cast in the same mould.
(Berry received a good character.)
BERRY — GUILTY . Aged 17.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18451027-2175]
2175. WILLIAM BAINES and WILLIAM REYNOLDS were indicted for a like offence.
LOUISA JULIAN . I keep a tobacconist's shop. On the 22nd of Sept. Baines came in for a pennyworth of tobacco—I served him with it—he paid me with a 4 d. piece—I did not look particularly at it—I had looked at the till shortly before—I had no silver but a shilling in it—as Baines stepped out the policeman stepped in, and in a hurried manner said, "What did that man pay in?"—I said, "A groat"—my son went and took it out of the till, bit it, and gave it to him.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. How did you put the 4 d. piece in the till? A. I opened the till and put it in—I saw there was only 1 s. in it—I examined it in one minute afterwards.
JOHN JULIAN . I am the son of Louisa Julian. I came into the shop directly Baines went out—the officer came in—I took the 4 d. piece out of the till and bit it, and handed it to him—there was only 1 s. in the till besides.
SARAH AITKIN . I am the wife of Charles Aitkin. We live in Great Suffolk-street, in the Borough—on the 22nd of Sept. Reynolds came into our shop for a 1 d. saveloy—he paid me with 4 d. piece—I put it into the till—the officer came in—I took it out and gave it him—I had no other silver in the till.
Reynolds. Q. Did you give me the change? A. I laid it on the counter—I do not know whether you had it.
JOHN WARD . I am barman at the Red Lion public-house at the corner of Great Sutton-street, Southwark. On the 22nd of Sept., about eight o'clock, Baines came in for half a pint of porter—he laid down a bad 4 d., piece—I bit it double, and very nearly broke it into two pieces—I returned it to him—he walked out with it, and left the beer on the counter.
Cross-examined. Q. How long was he in the shop? A. A minute or two—there were two or three other customers.
JOHN WHITLAM ( police-constable M 89.) I was on duty in the Old Kent-road in plain clothes with West—I saw the prisoners in the act of leaving the Kent-road and proceeding towards the Dover-road with another person—I saw Reynolds go to a grocer's shop—I followed them through the Dover-road to the Red Lion—Baines went in there—I saw him come out and join Reynolds and the other—they proceeded along Suffolk-street to the Windsor Castle—Baines went into Julian's shop, and Reynolds into the Windsor Castle—he came out in about a minute—Baines came out of Julian's shop, and I saw him put his right hand up to his hat twice, which I considered was a signal—they then joined company, and all proceeded to Aitkin's shop—Reynolds went in there—Baines and the other went on, and stood conversing at the corner of a dark turning—Baines then left the other, and passed and repassed Aitkin's shop—I took him into custody—I fouud upon him at the station a good sixpence—I
produce a 4 d. piece which I received from John Julian—I saw him take it out of the till.
THOMAS WEST ( police-constable M 249.) I was with Whitlam—I saw Reynolds go into Aitkin's shop—I followed him, inside—Baines had just passed—I could not see him when I was inside—I saw Reynolds take a 4 d. piece from his pocket, and put it on the counter—I told Mrs. Aitkin I thought it was had—she said she thought it was good—I said I was an officer, and I asked Reynolds if it was a bad one—Mrs. Aitkin took it and marked it, and gave it to me—Reynolds asked if there was no more money in the till—she took it out, and showed him there was no more—this is the 4 d. piece.
MR. JOHN FIELD . These are both counterfeit, and made of Britannia metal.
Baines. Mrs. Julian says I was in her shop, and had tobacco and change: I was taken soon after, and what could I have done with the tobacco or change?
BAINES — GUILTY of uttering to Julian.— Confined Six Months.
REYNOLDS *— GUILTY of uttering to Aitkin.— Confined Twelve Months.

2177. JANE JURY was indicted for stealing 3 3/4 yards of linen cloth, value 9 s.; 5 handkerchiefs, 3 s.; 3 blankets, 15 s.; and 76 yards of calico, 1 l. 6 s.; the goods of Thomas Shaw, her master.
MR. HUDDLESTON conducted the Prosecution.
RICHARD ROGERS . I am shopman to Thomas Shaw, linen-draper, High-street, Southwark—the prisoner was his cook. On the 18th of Oct., about ten o'clock at night, I went to the room occupied by myself—there is a spare bed in that room—as I went up stairs, I heard a rustling noise—I went into my room—there was no candle there, but I had one in my hand—I looked under the spare bed and saw the prisoner, and under the other bed I saw two parcels—I asked the prisoner who the first parcel belonged to—she said, to herself, and the other to Mrs. Cornelius—I opened the first parcel, and in it were three blankets, the property of my employer—I separated one of them from the other—it was one of a pair, of which I had sold one the day previous—in the other bundle I found fifty-three yards of calico, which was my employer's, and had his mark on—I then, with the officer, searched the prisoner's box, and found five cambric handkerchiefs, a length of lawn, and a piece or two of calico, which were my employer's, and had our private mark on them—these articles had not been sold—the prisoner was to have left on the Monday following.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Who is Mrs. Cornelius? A. A friend of the prisoner's, who came to the house occasionally.
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18451027-2178]
2178. ELLEN CONNOLLY was indicted for stealing 1 printed book, value 1 s. 6 d., the goods of William Shaw; and 10 yards of calico, 4 s., the goods of Thomas Shaw, her master.
MR. HUDDLESTON conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM SHAW . I am the son of Thomas Shaw—lie is a linen-draper—the prisoner was his housemaid. On Saturday night, the 18th of Oct., I, with a policeman, searched the prisoner's box—she was not present, but the policeman was, and Mr. Rogers—believe it was the prisoner's box—it was in a room adjoining her room, which is merely used to hold boxes and a chest of drawers—I found in the bottom of the box this book, which is mine—I also found three lengths of new calico, containing about ten yards—it was my father's, and had our private mark on it—I had missed my book about a month.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. I suppose the mark was on all the calico you sold? A. Yes—we have not had any young men left lately—the last who left was Mr. Thomas, about six months ago—he might have sold this calico—this is my book—the last time I saw it it was in the bookcase.
MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Has any other person left since the calico was in the stock? A. No—this was in the stock when Thomas left.
WILLIAM GRIST ( police-constable M 223). I was called in to Mr. Shaw's on the 18th of Oct.—after I took the prisoner to the station, I searched a box—I had asked her in the station for her keys—she said she had thrown them into a drawer—I found a chest of drawers in the lobby, where the boxes were—I found four keys in one drawer—I tried one to a box, and it opened it—found the book and the calico—the prisoner was in the station on the other charge—( see page 1033, No. 2177) the bookcase from which this book was taken was merely open shelves.
WILLIAM SHAW re-examine. Q. What was the cost of this book? A. I cannot remember—I had not left it about that anybody might have taken and read it—I had left it on the shelves.
WILLIAM ROGERS . I saw the box searched—it was the prisoner's—I know it from the position in which it was placed, and I had seen her locking and unlocking it.
Cross-examined. Q. How could you see the maid at her box? A. Our warehouse was opposite the room door, and I had to pass it several times a day—I do not know of her having bought any calico—the servants have bought things, I believe.
COURT to WILLIAM SHAW. Q. Where do you keep the calico? A. The lengths that are cut are below—the maids have to pass through there to fetch coals—these are each a fag end, and have our marks on—the prisoner had been a year and three-quarters or two years in my father's service, and was going away in consequence of our missing some spirits.
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury.— Confined Three Months.

2179. OWEN KEEFE and JOSEPH BRYAN were indicted for stealing 1 handkerchief, value 4 s., the property of William James Constable.
WILLIAM CONSTABLE . I live in Cherry Garden-street, Bermondsey. I was in Tooley-street one Wednesday night, in Sept., between eight and nine o'clock—in consequence of information I felt in my pocket, and missed my handkerchief, which I had used about a quarter of an hour before—this is it.
GEORGE ELLERY . I have lived in Burnham-street, Tooley-street, and I
am a ticket collector at the railway. On the evening of the 24th of Sept., I was at the top of Tooley-street—I had been speaking to the prosecutor, and saw the prisoners go behind him—I had not noticed a handkerchief in Keefe's hand previous to his going there, but I saw him after he went there hand a handkerchief to Bryan, which Bryan made his escape with.
Q. That is just the reverse of what you said before? A. I might make a mistake in the names—I had not seen either of them before—they were both stopped—Bryan threw down the handkerchief; I took it up and gave it to Jones.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. What time in the morning did you go to the police-office? A. It might be between twelve and one—I heard the names of the parties—I pointed out the person that I point out now, as having taken the handkerchief—it might be a mistake in the name—I think there were three boys beside the prisoners stood round—I cannot say how far Keefe went—Bryan went about nine or ten yards—I did not actually see Keefe take the handkerchief—I saw it in his hand—my deposition was read over to me, and I signed it.
Q. You say here, "I saw Bryan take a handkerchief from Mr. Constable's pocket" A. I did not say "candidly" that I saw him take it out of his pocket—he might have picked it up certainly—the prisoners might be a yard from the prosecutor when I first saw the handkerchief—neither of them were in front of him—I was standing looking straight it the prisoners—their backs were to me—I am positive one prisoner had it, and banded it to the other, who ran away.
EVAN JONES ( police-constable M 250). I produce this handkerchief, which I received from Ellary.
COURT to the Prosecutor. Q. What is your name? A. William James Constable—I said my name was William—it is—I heard from my father and mother that it was James—I go by the name of William James amongst my family.
Bryan. Mr. Taylor asked him three times if it was me took it, and he said it was me.
COURT to the Prosecutor. Q. Did that pass? A. No, I believe not—I have no recollection of it—Bryan was stopped by a man named Taylor, who is not here.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

2180. BENJAMIN CRUSSELL was indicted for stealing 3 pieces of iron, called weights, value 5 s.; 50 knives, 30 s.; 2 pairs of bellows, 5 s.; 6 benches, 3 l.; 1 vice, 10 s.; 1 lathe-wheel, 2 l.; 1 case for a beer-engine, 2 l.; and a grindstone, 2 s.; the goods of Charles Rice; and 2 iron melting-pans, 30 s.; and an iron furnace and chimney, 20 s.; the goods of Cbarles Rice, and fixed to a certain building.
MESSRS. BALLANTINE and BALDWIN conducted the Prosecution.
CHARLES RICE . I have been in business in the Borough for forty-fivs years, but I have now left it to my son—I am the owner of a house, No. 45, Roebuck-place, Dover-road, which I let to the prisoner at Ladyday last, at the rent of 45 l. a year—there was a detached building across the yard of that house, and that I was to let at 5 l. a year—I did not let that to the prisoner at all—I had a furnace and chimney and some other things
of mine in that building—some were fixed, and some moveable—they were to be kept in these back premises in case I should let the place to a pewterer—they were left there in consequence of the prisoner opening the house as a beer-shop—if that did not answer, I was to keep all these tools and utensils, thinking I might let it to a person in the same line again as it had been before—I did not tell the prisoner what he was to do with them—he was not to sell or dispose of them—they were to be left there as belonging to me, in case I should let the house, if his shop did not answer—I gave him no control or power over them at all—nothing was said about finding a purchaser—no authority was given to him in case any one should apply to him, to do anything—I said if any one came after the things, or wanted them, the person was to be sent to me—there was no other authority given by me to him, and the building was not let to him—I was to have 5 l. a year more if that was let, and I moved everything out of the shop to that—I considered the goods perfectly safe—in about two or three months I missed some of them—I made some inquiries, I think of the prisoner's wife—the prisoner remained my tenant in that house up to the second quarter-day, up to Michaelmas—he went away in the night—he gave me no notice of his leaving—he was gone on the quarter-day, and everything was cleared away—he did not, on or about the 8th of May, or between that and Michaelmas, tell me he had sold these goods, or anything of that sort—he did not pay me any rent.
JURY. Q. Was there any written agreement between you? Not that I know of—I cannot recollect signing any.
Cross-examined by MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Was this adjoining bulding in the yard of the premises? A. Yes, across the yard—there is a wall going round—it is a warehouse built at the end of the gardens—there is a lock upon it—I had the key of it in the first instance, but the prisoner asked for it, and then he had the key—I think he had the key soon after he had the place—he wanted to put some things into it—I did not allow him to use any of these articles—he was to take care that they were not taken away—he was in possession for my use, and to take care that no person took them away.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. After this house had been let to the prisoner, was any application made for the key of this out-house? A. Not that I recollect—the key was delivered to the prisoner some short time afterwards—he said to me that he wanted to put some things into it, and I banded him the key, believing he wanted to put some things in—I found two crates there of his—I did not miss the furnace nor the brick-work till after he was gone.
MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Was it after you had given him the key that you told him he was to keep these things for your use and not for his own? A. I never told him anything of the kind—they were to be taken care of in case I should let it to a pewterer again—there was no one in the house but him—they were certainly not to be taken care of for my use, but if I let it to a pewterer again—they were under the prisoner's protection, because he had the house—I do not know that they were under his protection for my use—they were for my use to sell again if I chose, to be disposed of to a pewterer—that understanding, about their being under his protection, was come to after I gave him the key.
COURT. Q. Was there anything said about them? A. No.
MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Do you mean nothing was said about these goods from beginning to end? A. No, there was nothing said about the furnace and chimneys—all the articles were placed in this outhouse—nothing was said to him about the articles that were placed in that out-house—there were some loose things, and them I fastened up in a box—I sent the box to lock them all up in, and they were not to be disturbed—he was not to let any other person have them.
Q. Were they not under his protection, though they were your property? A. When I lent him the key, I expected it to be returned; but when he had got the things locked up I did not ask him for it again—What Was there, was my property—he was not to disturb them—I did not tell him to take care of them—I did not at any time tell him they were to be taken care of for me—I did not tell him what was in the show him what was there—I did not remove the fixtures from the house to this place—I did not remove a single thing from the front house to this place—I do not know that they were put there by my orders—Was in the front shop was about a dozen and a half of pots and, some measures, that I allowed the prisoner to have to get the windows mended—I ask for the key afterwards when he had been a few days in possession of it—the key was in the door—I saw it in the door—I went in and saw all the things safe—when I came out I think I took the key—yes, I must have done so, or else how came he to ask me for it—after I had given him the key I asked him for it—I went and found it in the door—I left it in the door.
Q. You said you took the key? A. In first first instance I took the key, and then I think he had it again; but I found it in the door—my son was backwards and forwards, looking after the things—the prisoner got the key from me—he said he wanted to put some things in, and I found was two crates—I cannot charge my memory whether he asked me for that key after that occasion—it passed backwards and forwards two or three times—I cannot say what was the last occasion on which I gave him the key—it was to move out what he had got in—I did not ask him for the key after that—I considered the things were safe, and therefore did not ask him for it—I considered he was taking care of them—these premises had formerly been used for a pewteier—the things in this indictment were articles for the purpose of pewtering—the prisoner was a beer-shop keeper—he put up some things for the purpose of carrying on the Altonale business—there was not less than twenty squares of glass to be put in when he got into the house—he put some in—I agreed to allow him 1 s. a foot for the glass, and 6 d. a square for taking the old broken glass out—I did hot come after he had been in some time, ask him It he would dispose of any of the articles at the back of the shop—none at all—I know Mr. Barnett—he bought the things—I do not know Mr. Church or Mr. Paisley—I have seen Mr. Church within the last two or three days—I have seen Mr. paisley two or three days in Court—I have not told them that I authorised the prisoner to sell these articles for the best price he could get—to the best of my knowledge, I never saw them till I saw them in this Court—I did not call on the prisoner on the 2nd of May, to my knowledge—it might be on the 2nd of May—he did not tell me he could get 9 l. 10 s. for the things, if I approved of that sum, nor that if he were to sell these things I must allow him 3 l. 10 s. to paint the back and front windows, and to paint and grain the shutters—he did not paint the back
and front—I paid the bill for it in his shop—that was after he went in, to the best of my recollection—he did not tell me he had sold these things for twelve guineas, and he was to receive half in money and half in coppers—he told me he had got some coppers for sale, and if I knew any one who would buy them they should have them at 1 s. a pound, and I told him to let me have one to look at—he did not tell me he had sold them for twelve guineas, that he had half in money and half in coppers, and if I would take the coppers it would make up the rent—he was not to sell anything, but to send people to me—he did not sell any articles to Hudson—that was before he got into the house—I let the prisoner have part of the things in the front house, consisting of measures and brass taps, and other things, for mending the windows—they are not included in this indictment—I have an inventory of them in my pocket—I did not tell Mr. Barnett that I allowed the prisoner to sell the things, and he was to have 2 l. or 8 l. if he could do it—I did not tell Mr. Barnett, if he would not say anything about that when he came here on the trial, that I would not sue him for the money on the things—I declare to heaven I did not—I took possession of the premises when I found the prisoner was gone, about seven o'clock in the morning of the quarterday.
MR. BALDWIN. Q. The prisoner asked you for the key of this out-house to put some things in? A. Yes, for a lodger that he had got, and I saw two crates in it—they hid the furnace, and I could not see that it was gone—I cannot say whether the crates were placed one on the other or not—I ordered the box to go, and the things were secured in it—I had a great deal of conversation with Mr. Barnett about the things after I traced them—one was a great fly-wheel, seven feet in diameter—I had conversation with Mr. Barnett a fortnight after the house was left—I have seen him standing, here—I heard the prisoner had two executions in his house—at the time I gave him the key I did not give him any control over these things—I took it for granted he would not interfere with them—I do not remember precisely at what time he asked for the key—it might be a fort-night or three weeks after he had the premises—he put up some taps and vats for the Alton ale—he took everything away again—the house was gutted.
JAMES BARNETT . I am a coppersmith, with Mr. Pontifex—I carry on business in Dover-road. I know the prisoner—I purchased these things of him about the 8th of May—he came to me, and I went to the premises where these things were—he said he had purchased the fixtures and tools for 60 l., and he asked me twenty guineas for them—my foreman valued them, and I gave him 12 l. for them—I purchased them all at once.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you bad any conversation with Mr. Rice about this case? A. I have—he told me the prisoner was to sell them, and have a profit of 3 l., or something of that kind—I mentioned to the prosecutor that he had authorized the prisoner to sell them, and that he might get 2 l. or 3 l. by selling them.
COURT. Q. Did you tell Mr. Rice that he himself had said he authorized the prisoner to sell them? A. I said, "You authorized the prisoner to sell them, and be might get 2 l. or 3 l. by them;" he said so to me in my premises—when he first came to me about them he said he had authorized him to sell them, and he thought he might get 1 l. or 2 l. himself—I mentioned that afterwards to Mr. Rice when I went before the Grand Jury—he said it was false, he never said anything of the kind—I
said, "You have forgotten it, sir; you really did say so;" and he really said so.
JURY. Q. Do you recollect the former part of the conversation? A. I do not—he came to inquire if we had bought the things—I candidly told him we had, and then he said what I have stated.
MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Has Mr. Rice since that time promised not to sue you forthe value of the things if you would not say that? A. tanta-mount; to that—he begged, as a favour, that I would not say anything about it, because he wished to prosecute the man.
COURT. Q. Have I this right?—when Mr. Rice first came to you he said; "I authorised him to sell the things, and he might get 2 l. or 3 l. upon them? A. The tools, my Lord—I put him in mind of it before the Grand Jury, and he denied it, and said it was false—I said, "You may have forgotten it."
Q. And then you say he begged it as a favour that you would not say anything about it, as he wished to prosecute the man? A. Through another party, my Lord—Mr. Rice did not say that—he said the first part, that he authorized the prisoner.
MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Through whom did he say that? A. Through the policeman—church and Paisly are both in my employ—I mentioned publicly in my shop when I went from the Old Bailey what Mr. Rice had first said to me.
MR. BALDWIN. Q. Do you mean positively to swear that the prosecutor made use of that expression to you when he came about these things? A. He did—I can swear to it—It was not what the prisoner said—he said nothing of the kind—I am not confounding what the prisoner laid with what the prosecutor said—the prosecutor said he told him to sell the things, that he might get a pound or two by them himself—I am not aware that I ever mentioned that circumstance before—I was in the Police-court, but was not called on, or I should have stated it there——I stated it in the clerk's office—I made a deposition at the police-office—I was sworn to tell the whole truth, and nothing but the truth do not know that I stated one word about the prosecutor having said this to me—my deposition was read over to me, and I signed it in the clerk's office—I was sworn—I think it was in the clerk's office—the pr said he autliorised the prisoner to sell the tools—he did not say a word about the fixtures.
JOHN DALTON . I am the prosecutor's servant In April last, I went to these premises to pack up a lot of tools in the back workshop, is a building on the other side of the yard—I went into the building for that purpose—I put the tools into the chest, and corded it up—I did not see the prisoner—I saw wit wife.
cross-examined. Q. Did you take the key, or get it from the prisoner's wife? A. It was not locked, it was open—it was in the spring—I do not know the month—I took no key—I do not recollect the prisoner going into the house.
JOHN PURSER ( police-constable M 87.) I took the prisoner on this charge—he stated that he purchased the goods from Mr. Rice, and he did not consider Mr. Rice had anything to do with him.
Cross-examined. Q. Did not he say that That did not consider that Mr. Rice had any claim upon him, except for rent? A. I think he did—to the best of my belief I did not go from Mr. Rice to Mr. Barnett with any
message—I do not believe I did—I did not go to Mr. Barnett, and tell him not to mention what he had been told by Mr. Rice, as Mr. Rice wished to prosecute the prisoner—I went to Mr. Barnett last Thursday evening, after I left the Court to tell him what time to come in the morning—I did not say that Mr. Rice hoped he would not mention what he said to him, as he wished to prosecute the man—I swear I did not tell him anything else, except to tell him to be here in the morning—Mr. Rice gave me no message—I have spoken to him about this case several times—I did not tell him that I had heard he had told Mr. Barnett that he authorised the prisoner to sell these things—I have heard it whispered about—I have never told Mr. Rice, at any time; nor spoken to him About what he said, no more than I stated in my deposition, that the prisoner Said he bought them of Mr. Rice, and he did not consider he had anything to do with him.
MR. BALDWIN. Q. had Mr. Barnett been here on the Thursday? A. No, he had not.
MR. HUDDLESTON to MR. RICE. Q. Did you send any message by the policeman to Mr. Barnett? A. Certainly not; none whatever.
MR. HUDDLERTON called
JAMES DORE . I live in Lant-street, in the Borough—I have known the prisoner about two years—he has been an honest man as far as I have known and heard—I called at his house one morning, in May, and heard Mr. Rice drive up with his horse and gig, with footman with him—he got out of the gig, came in front of the bar, and said, "Mr. Crussell, have you sold those things of mine yet?"—crussell's reply was, "No, I have not yet, Sir"—he said, "I wish you would"—Mr. Crussell said, "I had an offer for them yesterday, and I can get 12 l. for them, half in money, and half in goods"—Mr. Rice's reply was, "What goods?"—he said, "Coppers"—Mr. Rice made a reply, and said, "I wish you would sell them, and do the best you can with them;" and he said, "Good morning," and went away—Mr. Crussell sard, "That was ray landlord."
MR. BALDWIN. Q. When did this take place? A. I do not know—it was some morning in May, from ten to eleven o'clock—I do not remember the day of the week, nor the day of the month—it was in the month of May—I would not say what day—I think about the 7th or 8th of May—I was in the habit of calling at Mr. Crussell's house, and other houses, for orders for cigars and tobacco on my own account—I saw Mr. Rice and his servant come there with a gig—I did not know Mr. Rice before, but Mr. Crussiell said that was his landlord—there was only me, and Mr. Rice, and the prisoner, and the footman, there, and the gig outside—this was in front of the bar in the Prince of Wales, in the Dover-road—there was no one else present—the prosecutor said, "Mr. Crussell, have you sold those things yet?"—these were the words—there was nothing said about fixtures—he said, "Those things in the back premises"—back premises were mentioned—I said the things in the back premises—Mr. Rice did not come in a carriage—it was a horse and two-wheeled gig, like what some of the doctors drive—I saw it.
Q. Just repeat what passed? A. Mr. Rice asked Crussell if he had sold the things in the back work-shop—I mean to swear those were the words used—I swear that I heard Mr. Rice ask Mr. Crussell whether he had sold those things in the workshop—Mr. Rice's words were, "Those things belonging tame in the back workshop"—I have been here attending
all the week—I have not spoken to the solicitor, or to any one—I came here merely to stale what I heard—I certainly did not tell any onwe that I came to state this—I thought these things had better e kept in, than to tell people outside—I have been outside whife this case was going on—I mentioned it to the man who keeps, the door—there have been a great many people outside with me—we have been all talking about it, and it is possible we have while the case was going on—I do not know how long the case has been going on—I did not tell the prisoner about it—I have never seen him from the time he left the Court at Union-hall till this time.
MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. The prisoner told, Mr. Rice he had had an offer of twelve guieas, half money aud half goods? A. Yes and he said, "Take and sell them, and do the best you can"—I stated this to the solicitor a week ago—I told him the same words I have stated now.
CHARLES SMITH . Iam a general dealer, and live at Camberwell. I was with Mr. Crussell when he sold these things to Mr. Barnett—I saw Mr. Rice at Mr. Crnssell's one morning, five or six months ago he came and asked Mr. Crussell if he had got those coppers for them—Mr. Crussell said he had not, he had been disappoisted about them; but he would go and see if he could get, them that same day—I did not hear about the other things.
MR. BALDWIN to CHRLES RICE. Q. Did you, May last go to the prisoner's house in a two-wheeled gig with your servant? A. I did not—I have a gig, but I did not see Dore, nor did I desire the prisoner, to sell this property.
COURT. Q. Did you go in any other carriage? A. I have called as I have gone by I have called repeatedly—I never went to ask the prisinor if he had sold the things—I called to see if the things we were safe when I heard what sort of a man had got there.
MR. HUDDLESTON Q. Did you see the things? A. I saw the things in the front place, and I saw the things that are stated—I was in and out several times—I passed the door, every day to go to the City—I drove my gig twelve months ago, but I did not in May—I could not undertake to swear I did not drive it in May—I do not think I did—I have put it on one side for some time—I think I did not—I did not drive it in May—I have a Clarence with four wheels a gig—I have two vehicles—I had not the gig in May, now I come to recollect, it was in the Isle of Thanet—to the best of my recollection I did not drive that gig or any gig in May—I think my gig was in the contry—I do not know that I can undertake to swear I did not drive it in May, it was so near a point when I had it away—I do not book these things—I think I may have said I did not drive it in May—I cannot tell indeed—I should rather say I did not drive it in May than that I did—I do not know—I might have driven it in May—I never saw Dore till I saw him here—I got one copper as a sample to sell for the prisoner, but that was on another concern.
COURT. Q. Did you get one copper from him? A. Yes, one as a sample—he said he had got a quantity to sell—he wanted 1 s. a pound for them—I said I knew a gentleman who was building some houses—it was to sell for the prisoner.
MR. BALDWIN to JOHN DALTON. Q. Are you in the habit of going out with your master in the gig or Clarence? A. Yes—I cannot say
which he drove in May—he has ridden in both since I have been with him, but not in the gig for some time.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you driven your master to the prisoner's house? A. We called there a great many times, but I do not recollect whether in the gig or in the Clarence.
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Confined Twelve Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
